\displaystyle~\E_{t\sim D_{U}}\Big[\frac{1}{TD_{U}(t)}|g_{z}(t)|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{U}\frac{1}{T}|g_{z}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|g_{z}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

(57)

where the first step is straight forward, the second step follows from the definition of
w_{i}
, the third step is straight forward, the forth step follows from the definition of expectation, the fifth step follows from
U\subseteq[0,T]
, the sixth step follows from the definition of the norm.

By Eq. (
57
) and Markov inequality, we have that with probability at least
0.9
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|g_{z}(t_{j})|^{2}\leq 20\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

(58)

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{j})|^{2}\geq

\displaystyle~0.5\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|z^{*}(t_{j})|^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|g_{z}(t_{j})|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~0.5\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|z^{*}(t_{j})|^{2}-20\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~0.2\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}-20\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~(0.2-20c)\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}

where the first step follows from
(a+b)^{2}\geq 0.5a^{2}-b^{2}
, the second step follows from Eq. (
58
), the third step follows from Lemma
J.6
, the forth step follows from
\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}
.

The total success probability follows from a union bound:
0.9-\rho>0.85
.

The lemma is then proved.
∎

The following lemma shows how to estimate the energy of a noisy local-test signal.

Lemma K.2
.

Let
x^{*}\in{\cal F}_{k,F}
be the ground-truth signal and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be the noisy observation signal.
Let
H
be defined as in Definition
E.5
,
{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
be defined as in Definition
C.2
with
(\sigma,b)
such that Large Offset event does not happen.
For any
j\in[B]
, suppose there exists an
f_{0}

with
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f_{0})
satisfying:

\displaystyle\int_{f_{0}-{\Delta_{h}}}^{f_{0}+{\Delta_{h}}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k,

where
\mathcal{N}^{2}\geq\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}
.
Let
z^{*}(t):=(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
and
z(t)=(x\cdot H)*{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
.
Let
g_{z}(t):=z(t)-z^{*}(t)
. Let
U=\{t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}~|~H(t)>1-\delta_{1}~\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\beta]\}
. Suppose that
\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}
, where
c\in(0,0.001)
is a small universal constant.

For
s\geq O(k\log(k)\log(1/\rho))
, let
S_{D_{U}}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{s}\}
be a set of i.i.d. samples from the distribution
D_{U}
defined as Eq. (
52
). Let
w_{i}=1/(TsD_{U}(t_{i}))
for
i\in[s]
.

Then, with probability at least
0.85
,

\|d_{z}(t)\|_{S_{D_{U}},w}^{2}\lesssim(c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}})\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2},

Proof.

We first consider the expectation of
\|g_{z}(t)e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t+\beta)\|_{S_{D_{U}},w}^{2}
.
We have that

\displaystyle~\E\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}|g_{z}(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{s}\E_{t_{i}\sim D_{U}}[w_{i}|g_{z}(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{s}\E_{t_{i}\sim D_{U}}\Big[\frac{1}{TsD_{U}(t_{i})}|g_{z}(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\E_{t\sim D_{U}}\Big[\frac{1}{TD_{U}(t)}|g_{z}(t)e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t+\beta)|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{U}\frac{1}{T}|g_{z}(t)e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t+\beta)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{4}{T}\int_{U}(|g_{z}(t)|^{2}+|g_{z}(t+\beta)|^{2})\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{8}{T}\int_{0}^{T}|g_{z}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~10\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2},

(59)

where the first step is straight forward, the second step follows from the definition of
w_{i}
, the third step is straight forward, the forth step follows from the definition of expectation, the fifth step follows from
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, the sixth step follows from
U\subseteq[0,T]
and
U+\beta\subseteq[0,T]
, the seventh step follows from the definition of the norm.

By Eq. (
59
) and Markov inequality, we have that with probability at least
0.9
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}|g_{z}(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}\leq 100\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

(60)

We have that

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{s}(2w_{i}|z^{*}(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z^{*}(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}+2w_{i}|g_{z}(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-g_{z}(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~200\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{s}2w_{i}|z^{*}(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z^{*}(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~200\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}+4\|z^{*}(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z^{*}(t)\|^{2}_{U}+2\sqrt{\delta_{1}}\|x(t)\|_{T}\|z^{*}(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z^{*}(t)\|_{U}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(200c+4\gamma^{2}+4\delta_{1})\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}+2\sqrt{\delta_{1}(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(c+\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}+\sqrt{\delta_{1}(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\frac{k}{\delta}}\|z^{*}(t)\|_{T}\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~((c+\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})+\sqrt{\delta_{1}(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\frac{k}{\delta}\frac{R-L}{T-k^{2}(T+L-R)}})\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}})\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}

where the first step follows from
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, the second step follows from Eq. (
60
), the third step follows from the partial energy estimation for local-test signal (Lemma
J.7
) which holds with probability
1-\rho
, the forth step follows from the
\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}
and Claim
K.8
, the fifth step follows from Lemma
H.3
, the sixth step follows from
[L,R]:=U
and Lemma
J.9
, the seventh step follows form
{R-L}\lesssim{T-k^{2}(T+L-R)}
,
\delta_{1}\delta^{-1}k\lesssim 1
.

The total success probability follows from a union bound
0.9-\rho>0.85
.

The lemma is then proved.
∎

K.2
Significant sample generation for a single bin

Recall that we define a sample
t\in[0,T]
is
significant
if the magnitude of the local-test signal at
t
is small, i.e.,
|d_{z}(t)|\leq O(|z(t)|)
. The following lemma shows that a significant sample can be efficiently generated, provided that the filtered noisy signal does not contain too much noise.

Lemma K.3

(Generate Significant samples for filtered noisy signals)
.

Let
x^{*}\in{\cal F}_{k,F}
be the ground-truth signal and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be the noisy observation signal.
Let
H
be defined as in Definition
E.5
,
{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
be defined as in Definition
C.2
with
(\sigma,b)
such that Large Offset event does not happen.
For any
j\in[B]
, suppose there exists an
f_{0}

with
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f_{0})
satisfying:

\displaystyle\int_{f_{0}-{\Delta_{h}}}^{f_{0}+{\Delta_{h}}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k,

where
\mathcal{N}^{2}\geq\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}
.
Let
z^{*}(t):=(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
and
z(t)=(x\cdot H)*{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
.
Let
g_{z}(t):=z(t)-z^{*}(t)
. Let
U=\{t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}~|~H(t)>1-\delta_{1}~\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\beta]\}
. Suppose that
\|g_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}
, where
c\in(0,0.001)
is a small universal constant.

Then, there is an algorithm
that takes
O(k\log(k))
samples in
z
, runs in
O(k\log(k))
time, and output an
\alpha\in U

such that with probability at least
0.6
,

|z(\alpha+\beta)-z(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq O({c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}}})|z(\alpha)|^{2}\leq 0.01|z(\alpha)|^{2}.

Proof.

The output
\alpha
is sample in two steps:

1.

For
s\geq O(k\log(k))
, generate
s
i.i.d. samples
S_{D_{U}}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{s}\}
bfrom the distribution
D_{U}
defined as Eq. (
52
). Let
w_{i}=1/(TsD_{U}(t_{i}))
for
i\in[s]
be the weights.

2.

Define a probability distribution
D_{S}
such that

\displaystyle D_{S}(t_{i}):=\frac{w_{i}|z(t_{i})|^{2}}{\sum_{i\in[s]}w_{i}|z(t_{i})|^{2}}~~~\forall i\in[s].

(61)

And sample
\alpha
according to
D_{S}
.

The sample and time complexities of this procedure are straightforward. It remains to prove that
\alpha
satisfies the significance requirement stated in the lemma.

By Lemma
K.1
, we have that with probability at least 0.85,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{j})|^{2}\geq(0.2-20c)\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}

(62)

By Lemma
K.2
, we have that with probability at least 0.85,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}\lesssim(c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}})\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}

(63)

Thus, with probability at least 0.7,

\displaystyle~\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{j})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{O(c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}})\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{j})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{O(c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}})\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}}{(0.2-20c)\cdot\|z^{*}(t)\|_{U}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O({c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}}})

(64)

where the first step follows from Eq. (
63
), the second step follows from Eq. (
62
), the third step is straight forward.

For a random sample
\alpha\sim D_{S}
, we bound the following expectation:

\displaystyle~\E_{\alpha\sim D_{S}}\left[\frac{|z(\alpha)e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z(\alpha+\beta)|^{2}}{|z(\alpha)|^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i=1}^{s}\frac{w_{i}|z(t_{i})|^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{j}|z(t_{j})|^{2}}\cdot\frac{|z(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{|z(t_{i})|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{s}w_{i}|z(t_{i})e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z(t_{i}+\beta)|^{2}}{\sum_{j=1}^{s}w_{j}|z(t_{j})|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O({c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}}}),

where the first step follows from the definition of
D_{m}
, the second step is straightforward, the third step follows from Eq. (
64
).

Thus by Markov inequality, with probability
0.9
,

\displaystyle\frac{|z(\alpha)e^{2\pi if_{0}\beta}-z(\alpha+\beta)|^{2}}{|z(\alpha)|^{2}}\leq\frac{O({c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}}})}{0.1}=O({c+\sqrt{\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}}}).

The success probability follows from a union bound. And the second inequality follows from the range of the parameters
c,\gamma,\delta_{1}
.
∎

K.3
Significant sample generation for multiple bins

In this section, we present our significant sample generation procedure that simultaneously works for all “good bins”.

We first prove the correctness of Algorithm
2
.

Lemma K.4

(Generate significant samples for different bins simultaneously)
.

Let
x^{*}\in{\cal F}_{k,F}
be the ground-truth signal and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be the noisy observation signal.
Let
H
be defined as in Definition
E.5
,
{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
be defined as in Definition
C.2
with
(\sigma,b)
such that Large Offset event does not happen.
Let
U=\{t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}~|~H(t)>1-\delta_{1}~\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\beta]\}
.

For
j\in[B]
, let
z_{j}^{*}(t):=(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
and
z_{j}(t)=(x\cdot H)*{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
.
Let
g_{j}(t):=z_{j}(t)-z_{j}^{*}(t)
.
Let

\displaystyle S_{g1}:=\big\{j\in[B]~|~\|g_{j}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|z^{*}_{j}(t)\|_{U}^{2}\big\},

where
c\in(0,0.001)
is a small universal constant.
Let

\displaystyle S_{g2}:=\left\{j\in[B]~\Bigg|~\exists f_{0},h_{\sigma,b}(f_{0})=j~\text{and}~\int_{f_{0}-{\Delta_{h}}}^{f_{0}+{\Delta_{h}}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k\right\},

where
\mathcal{N}^{2}\geq\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}
. Let
S_{g}=S_{g1}\cap S_{g2}
.

There is a Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
(Algorithm
2
) that takes
O(k^{2}\log^{2}(k/\delta_{1}))
samples in
x
, runs in
O(k^{2}\log^{3}(k/\delta_{1}))
time, and for each
j\in S_{g}
, output
\alpha_{j}
such that with probability at least
0.6
,

|z_{j}(\alpha_{j}+\beta)-z_{j}(\alpha_{j})e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq 0.01|z_{j}(\alpha_{j})|^{2}~~~\forall j\in S_{g}.

Proof.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, define a probability distribution
D(t)
as follows:

\displaystyle D(t):=\begin{cases}{c}\cdot(1-|t/T|)^{-1}T^{-1},&\text{ for }|t|\leq T(1-{1}/k)\wedge t\in U\\
c\cdot kT^{-1},&\text{ for }|t|\in[T(1-{1}/k),T]\wedge t\in U\end{cases}

where
c=\Theta(\log(k)^{-1})
is a normalization factor such that
\int_{-T}^{T}D(t)\mathrm{d}t=1
. For any
\varepsilon,\rho\in(0,1)
, let
S_{D}=\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{s}\}
be a set of i.i.d. samples from
D(t)
of size
s\geq O(k\log(k)\log(1/\rho))
. Let the weight vector
w\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
be defined by
w_{i}:=1/(TsD(t_{i}))
for
i\in[s]
.

Suppose all the bins can access the same set of time points
S_{D}
. Then, by Lemma
K.3
, for any
j\in S_{g}
with probability
0.6
, we have that,

\displaystyle|z_{j}(\alpha+\beta)-z_{j}(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq 0.01|z_{j}(\alpha)|^{2}.

Then, we show that the value of
z_{j}(t),j=1,\cdots,B
of same set of time points
S_{D}
can be compute by accessing a same set of time points in
x(t)
. By Lemma
D.2
with setting
a=\alpha/\sigma
, we have that

\displaystyle z_{j}(\alpha)=\widehat{u}_{j},

which is computed by the algorithm.

As a result, for each
j\in S_{g}
,

\displaystyle|z_{j}(\alpha_{j}+\beta)-z_{j}(\alpha_{j})e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq 0.01|z_{j}(\alpha_{j})|^{2}~~~\forall j\in S_{g},

holds with probably
0.6
.

∎

We compute the time and sample complexities of Algorithm
2
in the following two lemmas.

Lemma K.5

(Running time of Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
)
.

Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
runs in
O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))
times.

Proof.

In each call of Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
,

•

In line
5
, taking
|S|
samples runs
O(|S|)
times.

•

In line
6
, the for loop repeats
|S|
times,

–

In line
7
, the for loops repeats
B
times and
j
iterate from
1
to
B
, in each loop line
9
and line
10
, computing the summation runs in
|\{j+iB|i\in\mathbb{Z}\wedge j+iB\in\mathrm{supp}(G)\}|
times.

–

In line
12
and
13
, running Fast Fourier Transform algorithm takes
O(B\log(B))
time, where
B
is the length of the vector
u
and
u^{\beta}
.

–

In line
14
, the for loop repeats
B
times, each loop runs in
O(1)
times.

•

In line
19
, assigning
w_{i}
runs in
|S|
times.

•

In line
20
, computing
\sum_{t_{i}\in S}w_{i}|z_{j}(t_{i})|^{2}
runs in
|S|
times.

•

In line
22
, the for loop repeats
B
times, each loop runs in
O(1)
times.

Notice that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[B]}|\{j+iB|i\in\mathbb{Z}\wedge j+iB\in\mathrm{supp}(G)\}|\leq|\mathrm{supp}(G)|.

In the algorithm, we set the parameters:

\displaystyle B=~O(k),~~\text{and}~~~|S|=~k\log(k).

(65)

Thus,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{supp}(G)|=O(lB/\alpha)=k\log(k/\delta_{1}),

(66)

where the first step follows from Lemma
C.4
Property IV, the second step follows from
\alpha\eqsim 1
and
l=\Theta(\log(k/\delta_{1}))
.

Therefore, the time complexity in total is

\displaystyle~O(O(|S|)+|S|\cdot(|\mathrm{supp}(G)|+O(B\log(B))+B\cdot O(1))+|S|+|S|+B\cdot O(1))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(|S|\cdot(|\mathrm{supp}(G)|+B\log(B)))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k\log(k)\cdot(|\mathrm{supp}(G)|+k\log(k)))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k\log(k)\cdot(k\log(k/\delta_{1})+k\log(k)))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})),

where the first step is straightforward, the second step follows from Eq. (
65
), the third step follows from Eq. (
66
), the forth step is straight forward.
∎

Lemma K.6

(Sample complexity of Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
)
.

Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
takes
O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))
samples.

Proof.

In each call of Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
,

•

In line
6
, the for loop repeats
|S|
times,

•

In line
7
, the for loops repeats
B
times and
j
iterate from
1
to
B
, in each loop line
9
and line
10
, computing the summation takes
O(|\{\sigma(a-j-iB)|i\in\mathbb{Z}\wedge j+iB\in\mathrm{supp}(G)\}|)
samples.

Following from the setting in the algorithm, we have that

\displaystyle|S|=

\displaystyle~k\log(k).

(67)

Thus,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{supp}(G)|=O(lB/\alpha)=k\log(k/\delta_{1}).

(68)

where the first step follows from Lemma
C.4
Property IV, the second step follows from
\alpha\eqsim 1
and
l=\Theta(\log(k/\delta_{1}))
.

So, the samples complexity of Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
is

\displaystyle~|S|\cdot\sum_{j\in[B]}O(|\{\sigma(a-j-iB)~|~i\in\mathbb{Z}\wedge j+iB\in\mathrm{supp}(G)\}|)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(|S|\cdot|\mathrm{supp}(G)|)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(|S|\cdot k\log(k/\delta_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k\log(k)\cdot k\log(k/\delta_{1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))

where the first step is straight forward, the second step follows from Eq. (
68
), the third step follows from Eq. (
67
), the forth step is straight forward.
∎

K.4
Technical claims

We prove two technical claims in below about the local-test signals’ energy reduction.

Claim K.7

(Energy decay of local-test signals)
.

Let
x^{*}\in{\cal F}_{k,F}
. For any
(\sigma,b)
such that Large Offset event does not happen and any
j\in[B]
, suppose there exists an
f_{0}
with
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f_{0})
satisfying: well-isolation conditions and

\displaystyle\int_{f_{0}-{\Delta_{h}}}^{f_{0}+{\Delta_{h}}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k,

where
\mathcal{N}^{2}\geq\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}
. Let
z(t)=(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
be the filtered signal.

For
\beta\leq\gamma/\Delta_{0}
with
\Delta_{0}=O(\Delta)
, let
d_{z}(t)=z(t)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}-z(t+\beta)
be the local-test signal. We have that

\displaystyle\|d_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\cdot\|z(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Proof.

Let
S:=\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{x}^{*}*\widehat{H})
be the support set of
\widehat{x}^{*}*\widehat{H}
. Let

\displaystyle V:=\{f\in\mathbb{R}~|~\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)\geq 1-\delta_{1}\}.

Note that
\|d_{z}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
can be expressed as follows (ignoring the
\frac{1}{T}
factor):

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|z(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|z(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{z}(f)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{S}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{S\cap V}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f+\int_{S\backslash V}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f,

where the first step is straight forward, the second step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the third step is straight forward, the forth step follows from the assumption that Large Offset event does not happen, the fifth step is straight forward.

Then, for the first term, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{S\cap V}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{0}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{0}}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{0}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{0}}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot\gamma^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\gamma^{2}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\gamma^{2}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\gamma^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

(69)

where the first step follows from
S\cap V\subset[f_{0}-\Delta_{0},f_{0}+\Delta_{0}]
by Claim
D.3
, the second step follows from

|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|\leq 4\pi\beta|f-f_{0}|\leq 4\pi\beta{\Delta_{h}}_{0}\lesssim\gamma,

the third step is straight forward, the forth step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the fifth step follows from Lemma
G.3
.

For the second term, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{S\backslash V}|\widehat{z}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f=

\displaystyle~\int_{S\backslash V}|(\widehat{x}^{*}*\widehat{H})(f)|^{2}\cdot|\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{S\backslash V}|(\widehat{x}^{*}*\widehat{H})(f)|^{2}\cdot\delta_{1}^{2}\cdot|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\int_{S\backslash V}|(\widehat{x}^{*}*\widehat{H})(f)|^{2}\cdot\delta_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{x}^{*}*\widehat{H})(f)|^{2}\cdot\delta_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\delta_{1}^{2}\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(x^{*}\cdot H)(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\delta_{1}^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|x^{*}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

(70)

where the first step follows from the definition of
z
, the second step follows from the definition of
V
, the third step follows from
|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f\beta}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\lesssim 1
, the forth step is straight forward, the fifth step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the sixth step follows from Lemma
E.9
Property IV and V.

Putting them together, we get that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}|z(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim

\displaystyle~\gamma^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\delta_{1}^{2}\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|x(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1}^{2}\delta^{-1}k)\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\cdot\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

where the first step follows from Eq. (
69
) and Eq. (
70
), the second step follows from Lemma
H.3
, the last step follows from
\delta_{1}\delta^{-1}k\lesssim 1
.

The proof of the lemma is then completed.
∎

Similar result also holds for the partial energy:

Claim K.8

(Partial energy decay of local-test signals)
.

Let
x^{*}(t)
be a
k
-Fourier-sparse signal. Let
z(t):=(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
and
d_{z}(t)=z(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z(t)
. Let
U=\{t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}~|~H(t)>1-\delta_{1}~\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\beta]\}=:[L,R]
. For
\beta\leq\gamma/\Delta_{0}
with
\Delta_{0}=O(\Delta)
, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{L}^{R}|d_{z}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\lesssim(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\cdot\int_{L}^{R}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\int_{L}^{R}|z(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|z(t+\beta)-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\cdot z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\int_{0}^{T}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\frac{T}{T-k^{2}(T+L-R)}\int_{L}^{R}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~(\gamma^{2}+\delta_{1})\int_{L}^{R}|z(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

where the first step is straight forward, the second step follows from Claim
K.7
, the third follows from Lemma
J.9
with time duration changed from
[-T,T]
to
[0,T]
, the forth step follows from
{T-k^{2}(T+L-R)}\gtrsim T
.
∎

Appendix L
Frequency Estimation

We introduce our improved frequency estimation algorithm in this section. We first show that given significant samples, we are able to estimate a specific target frequency (see Section
L.1
). Then, we show to generalize it to simultaneously estimate frequencies for multiple bins and give our main frequency estimation algorithm (see Section
L.2
). Next, we prove several technical claims on the votes distribution in the
ArySearch
procedure (see Section
L.3
).

L.1
Frequency estimation via significant samples

In this section, we show an algorithm such that for a target frequency
f_{0}
, it can use several significant samples to estimate it with high accuracy.
The main idea is as follows: for a significant sample
\alpha
, since
|z(\alpha+\beta)-z(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|
is very small, the angle of
\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)}
will be close to
2\pi f_{0}\beta
. That is,

\displaystyle\arg\Big(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)}\Big)\approxeq 2\pi f_{0}\beta\pmod{2\pi}.

Solving the congruence equation gives that

\displaystyle f_{0}\approx\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}\Big(\arg\Big(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)}\Big)+2\pi s\Big)

for some unknown
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.

To find the unknown
s
, we use the same strategy as in
[
26
]
: perform a
D
-round searching procedure to narrow the possible range of
f_{0}
. More specifically, at the beginning, the possible range of
f_{0}
(frequency interval) is
[-F,F]
.
And after
D
rounds,
f_{0}
is located in a frequency interval of length
O(\Delta)
, resulting in an estimate with
\Delta
accuracy.

For
d\in[D]
, consider the
d
-th round of searching, where the frequency interval is:

\displaystyle[\mathsf{left}_{d},\mathsf{left}_{d}+\mathsf{len}_{d}].

We equally partition the frequency interval into
\mathsf{num}
parts and do a
\mathsf{num}
-ary search. We generate
R
significant samples, and for each sample
\alpha
, we enumerate all possible
s
and compute

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}\Big(\arg\Big(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)}\Big)+2\pi s\Big).

Then, we find which part this quantity falls in and add a vote to that part. For robustness, we also add votes to that part’s left and right neighbors. In the end, the frequency interval for the next round is the part with more than
R/2
votes. It is easy to see that at most
5
parts can be selected in the new frequency interval.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{len}_{d+1}\leq\frac{\mathsf{len}_{d}}{\mathsf{num}/5},

(71)

i.e., the length of the possible range of
f_{0}
decays at a constant rate.

More formally, we have the following lemma:

Lemma L.1

(significant sample to frequency estimation)
.

Suppose that there is an algorithm
GetSignificantSample
that

•

takes
z(t),\beta
as input where
\beta\leq O(1/\Delta)
,

•

takes
{\cal S}
samples in
z(t)
,

•

runs in
{\cal T}
time,

•

outputs an
\alpha
such that with probability
0.9
,

|z(\alpha+\beta)-z(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq 0.0001|z(\alpha)|^{2}.

Then, there is an Procedure
FrequencyEstimationZ
in Algorithm
3
that

•

takes
O(\log(FT)\log(\log(FT))\log(1/\rho_{1}){\cal S})
samples,

•

runs in
O(\log(FT)\log(\log(FT))\log(1/\rho_{1}){\cal T})
times,

•

and outputs
\widetilde{f}_{0}
such that with probability at least
1-\rho_{1}
,

\displaystyle|\widetilde{f}_{0}-f_{0}|\lesssim\Delta.

Proof.

We prove the correctness, time/sample complexity of Algorithm
3
in below.

Correctness:

We first compute the value of
D
, the number of rounds needed for the searching procedure. Note that the
GetSignificantSample
procedure requires that
\beta\leq O(1/\Delta)
. In our algorithm, we take
\beta_{d}=O(\mathsf{num}/\mathsf{len}_{d})
for the
d
-th round. Hence, for the last round
d=D
, we have that,

\displaystyle O(\mathsf{num}/\mathsf{len}_{D})=O(1/\Delta)~~\Longrightarrow~~\mathsf{len}_{D}\geq\mathsf{num}\Delta.

Then, by Eq. (
71
) and
\mathsf{len}_{1}=F
, we get that

\displaystyle D=\log_{\mathsf{num}}(\frac{FT}{\mathsf{num}(T\Delta)})\lesssim\log(FT)/\log(\mathsf{num}).

(72)

Then, we calculate the success probability. For
d\in[D]
, by Claim
L.8
, with probability at least
1-O(c+\rho)^{R/6}
, the true part containing
f_{0}
and its left and right neighbor will get
R
votes in total, and the other far away parts will get at most
R/2
votes. In this case, the new frequency interval will contain the true part, and we consider this round being success.
Since the search procedure takes
D
rounds, by a union bound, all rounds will succeed with probability at least

\displaystyle 1-D\cdot O(c+\rho)^{R/6}\geq

\displaystyle~1-\frac{\log(FT)}{\log(\mathsf{num})}\cdot O(c+\rho)^{R/6}\geq 1-\rho_{1}

where the first step follows from Eq. (
72
), the second step follows from

\displaystyle R\geq O\Big(\frac{\log(\log(FT)/\rho_{1})}{\log(1/(c+\rho))}\Big)\geq O\Big(\frac{\log(\log(FT)/(\rho_{1}\log(\mathsf{num})))}{\log(1/(c+\rho))}\Big).

Therefore, with probability at least
1-\rho
, the final frequency interval of length
O(\Delta)
will contain the target frequency
f_{0}
, which means that the output
\widetilde{f}_{0}
satisfies
|\widetilde{f}_{0}-f_{0}|\lesssim\Delta
. And the correctness of the algorithm is proved.

Time complexity:

We show that Procedure
FrequencyEstimationZ
in Algorithm
3
runs in
O(\log(FT)\cdot\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))
times.

In each call of the Procedure
FrequencyEstimationZ
in Algorithm
3
,

•

The for-loop repeats
D
times.

•

In each loop, line
6
call Procedure
ArySearch
.

Then, in the
d
-th call of the Procedure
ArySearch
,

•

In line
12
, the for-loop repeats
R
times.

•

In line
13
, the Procedure
GetSignificantSample
is called.

•

In line
14
, the for-loop repeats
\beta_{d}\mathsf{len}_{d}+O(1)
times.

•

In line
16
, the for-loop repeats
\mathsf{num}
times.

Thus, the total time complexity is dominated by:

\displaystyle D\cdot R\cdot(\beta_{d}\mathsf{len}_{d}+O(1))\cdot\mathsf{num}+D\cdot R\cdot{\cal T}.

By the parameter settings in Algorithm
3
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{num}=

\displaystyle~O(1),

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta})/\log(\mathsf{num})),

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{\log(FT)}{\rho_{1}\log(\mathsf{num})})),

\displaystyle\beta_{d}=

\displaystyle~O(\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}_{d}}),

In particular, we have

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta})/\log(\mathsf{num}))\leq O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta}))\leq O(\log(FT)),

where the first step follows from the setting of
D
, the second step follows from
\mathsf{num}=O(1)
, the third step follows from
\Delta=\mathrm{poly}(k)
.

Hence, the total time complexity of Algorithm
3
is

\displaystyle~O(D\cdot R\cdot(\beta_{d}\mathsf{len}_{d}+O(1))\cdot\mathsf{num})+D\cdot R\cdot{\cal T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(D\cdot R\cdot(O(\mathsf{num})+O(1))\cdot\mathsf{num})+D\cdot R\cdot{\cal T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(D\cdot R\cdot{\cal T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(\log(FT)\cdot\log(\log(FT)/\rho_{1})\cdot{\cal T}),

where the first step follows from
\beta_{d}=O(\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}_{d}})
, the second step follows from
\mathsf{num}=O(1)
, the third step follows from the choices of
D
and
R
.

Sample complexity:

Each call of the Procedure
GetSignificantSample
takes
{\cal S}
samples, and it is called
DR
times. Thus, the total sample complexity of Algorithm
3
is

\displaystyle DR\cdot{\cal S}=O(\log(FT)\cdot\log(\log(FT)/\rho_{1})\cdot{\cal S}).

The proof of the lemma is completed.

∎

Simultaneously estimate frequencies for different bins

.

Theorem L.2

(Better frequency estimation algorithm)
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be the observation signal where
g(t)
is arbitrary noise. Let
\Delta_{h}:=O(|\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{H})|)
,
\Delta:=O(k\cdot\Delta_{h})
and
\mathcal{N}^{2}:=\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
. Let
H
be defined as in Definition
E.5
,
{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
be defined as in Definition
C.2
with
(\sigma,b)
such that Large Offset event does not happen.
Let
U=\{t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}~|~H(t)>1-\delta_{1}~\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\beta]\}
.

For
j\in[B]
, let
z_{j}^{*}(t):=(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
and
z_{j}(t)=(x\cdot H)*{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
.
Let
g_{j}(t):=z_{j}(t)-z_{j}^{*}(t)
. Let

\displaystyle S_{g1}=\{j\in[B]~|~\|g_{j}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|z^{*}_{j}(t)\|_{U}^{2}\},

where
c\in(0,0.001)
is a small universal constant.
Let

\displaystyle S_{g2}=\Big\{j\in[B]~\Big|~\exists f_{0},h_{\sigma,b}(f_{0})=j~\text{and}~\int_{f_{0}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{0}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k\Big\}.

Let
S_{g}=S_{g1}\cap S_{g2}
. Let
S_{f}=\{f_{i}~|~\exists j\in S_{g}:h_{\sigma,b}(f_{i})=j~\forall i\in[k]\}
.

There is a Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
in Algorithm
5
such that:

•

takes
k^{2}\log(1/\delta)\log(FT)
samples,

•

runs in
k^{2}\log(1/\delta)\log^{2}(FT)
time,

•

returns a set
L
of
O(k)
frequencies such that with probability
1-\rho_{0}
, for any
f\in S_{f}
, there exists an
\widetilde{f}\in L
satisfying

|f-\widetilde{f}|\lesssim\Delta.

Proof.

We prove the correctness, time complexity, and sample complexity of Algorithm
5
in below.

Correctness:

By Lemma
K.4
, we know that the Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
in Algorithm
2
takes
{\cal S}=O(k^{2}\log^{2}(k/\delta_{1}))
samples in
x
, runs in
{\cal T}=O(k^{2}\log^{3}(k/\delta_{1}))
time, and for each
j\in S_{g}
, and outputs
\alpha_{j}
such that for each
j\in S_{g}
with probability
0.6
,

|z_{j}(\alpha_{j}+\beta)-z_{j}(\alpha_{j})e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq 0.01|z_{j}(\alpha_{j})|^{2},

where
f_{0}
satisfies

\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k,

(73)

and
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f_{0})
.

In the line
4
, we call the algorithm
FrequencyEstimationZ
(x,H,G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b})
. By Lemma
L.1
,
FrequencyEstimationZ
(x,H,G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b})
output
\widetilde{f}
for each
f_{j}\in S_{f}
such that with probability at least
1-\rho_{1}

\displaystyle|\widetilde{f}-f_{j}|\lesssim\Delta.

As a result, for all the
f\in S_{f}
, there is a
\widetilde{f}\in L
such that

\displaystyle|\widetilde{f}-f|\lesssim\Delta

holds with probability at least

\displaystyle 1-B\rho_{1}\geq 1-\rho_{0}.

Time complexity:

We show that the Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
in Algorithm
5
runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

time.

In each call of the Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
in Algorithm
5
,

•

Line
2
call Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
, which runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

time by Lemma
L.3
.

•

The for-loop repeats
B
times:

–

In each loop, line
4
call Procedure
FrequencyEstimationZ
, which runs in

O(\log(FT)\cdot\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

time by Lemma
L.1
.

Thus, the total time complexity is

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))+B\cdot O(\log(FT)\cdot\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))+O(k)\cdot O(\log(FT)\cdot\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}})),

where the first step follows from
B=O(k)
, the second step is straight forward.

Sample complexity:

We show that the Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
in Algorithm
5
takes

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

samples.

In each call of the Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
in Algorithm
5
, Line
2
call Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
, which takes
O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))
samples by Lemma
L.4
.

So, the sample complexity of Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
in Algorithm
5
is

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}})).

∎

The following two lemmas shows the time complexity and sample complexity of the significant sample generation procedure in Algorithm
4
.

Lemma L.3

(Running time of Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
4
)
.

Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
4
runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

times.

Proof.

In each call of Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
4
, in line
3
, the for loop repeats
D
times, in line
6
, the for loops repeats
R
times,

•

In line
8
, by Lemma
K.5
, each call of Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
takes
O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))
times.

•

In line
9
, the for loop repeats
B
times, each iteration runs in
O(1)
times.

Following from the setting in the algorithm, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{num}=

\displaystyle~O(1),

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta})/\log(\mathsf{num})),

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{\log(FT)}{\rho_{1}\log(\mathsf{num})})),

\displaystyle B=

\displaystyle~O(k).

(74)

We have that

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta})/\log(\mathsf{num}))\leq O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta}))\leq O(\log(FT)),

(75)

where the first step follows from the setting of
D
, the second step follows from
\mathsf{num}=O(1)
, the third step follows from
\Delta=\mathrm{poly}(k)
.

We also have that

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{\log(FT)}{\rho_{1}\log(\mathsf{num})}))\leq O(\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}})),

(76)

where the first step follows from the setting of
R
, the second step follows from
\mathsf{num}=O(1)
.

So, the time complexity of Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
4
is

\displaystyle~D\cdot R\cdot(O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))+B\cdot O(1))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\log(FT))\cdot R\cdot(O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))+B\cdot O(1))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\log(FT))\cdot O(\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))\cdot(O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))+B\cdot O(1))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\log(FT))\cdot O(\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))\cdot(O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))+O(k)\cdot O(1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}})),

where the first step follows from Eq. (
75
), the second step follows from Eq. (
76
), the third step follows from Eq. (
74
), the forth step is straightforward.

∎

Lemma L.4

(Sample complexity of Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
4
)
.

Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
4
takes

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

samples.

Proof.

In each call of Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
4
, In line
3
, the for loop repeats
D
times, in line
6
, the for loops repeats
R
times,

•

In line
8
, by Lemma
K.5
, each call of Procedure
GenerateSignificantSamples
takes
O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))
samples.

Following from the setting in the algorithm, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{num}=

\displaystyle~O(1),

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta})/\log(\mathsf{num})),

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{\log(FT)}{\rho_{1}\log(\mathsf{num})})).

We have that

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta})/\log(\mathsf{num}))\leq O(\log(\frac{FT}{\Delta}))\leq O(\log(FT)),

(77)

where the first step follows from the setting of
D
, the second step follows from
\mathsf{num}=O(1)
, the third step follows from
\Delta=\mathrm{poly}(k)
.

We also have that

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle~O(\log(\frac{\log(FT)}{\rho_{1}\log(\mathsf{num})}))\leq O(\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}})),

(78)

where the first step follows from the setting of
R
, the second step follows from
\mathsf{num}=O(1)
.

So, the sample complexity of Procedure
SamplingSignificantSample
in Algorithm
