is

\displaystyle~D\cdot R\cdot O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\log(FT))\cdot R\cdot O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\log(FT))\cdot O(\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))\cdot O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}})),

where the first step follows from Eq. (
77
), the second step follows from Eq. (
78
), the third step is straightforward.

∎

L.3
Vote distributions in
ArySearch

In this section, we prove several claims on the distributions of votes when we perform the
\mathsf{num}
-ary search on the frequency interval.

We first consider a single voter, i.e., one significant sample.
The following claim shows that, if
f_{0}
is in the
q
-th part, then this part or its left neighbor or its right neighbor will get at least one vote.

Claim L.5
.

For
\mathsf{len}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{num}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},q\in[1,\mathsf{num}]
,
let
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]
.
For any
\beta\in[\frac{c}{2}\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}},c\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}}]
with constant
c\in(0,0.01)
, for any constant
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01\cdot c^{2})
,
let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle|z(\alpha+\beta)-z(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq\varepsilon|z(\alpha)|^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle\Theta=\Big\{\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s)~\Big|~s\in[\beta\mathsf{left}-10,\beta(\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len})+10]\cap\mathbb{Z}\Big\}.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\Theta\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big)\right|=1.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)}-e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}\Big|\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

Since
|e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|=1
and
\sin(x)\approx x
for small
x
, it indicates that

\displaystyle\Big\|\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})-2\pi f_{0}\beta\Big\|_{\bigcirc}\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon},

(79)

where
\|a\|_{\bigcirc}=\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}}{\min}~|a+2\pi x|
. Thus, Eq. (
79
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}}{\min}~\Big|\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})-2\pi f_{0}\beta+2\pi x\Big|\lesssim\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

(80)

Let
s_{0}
be defined as

\displaystyle s_{0}:=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}~\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})-2\pi f_{0}\beta+2\pi x.

We first show that
s_{0}
falls in interval in the definition of
\Theta
. We have that

\displaystyle|2\pi s_{0}-2\pi f_{0}\beta|\leq

\displaystyle~\Big|\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})-2\pi f_{0}\beta+2\pi s_{0}\Big|+\Big|\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\Big|\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})-2\pi f_{0}\beta+2\pi s_{0}\Big|+2\pi

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~2\pi+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

(81)

where the first step follows from the triangle inequality, the second step follows from
|\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})|\leq 2\pi
, the third step follows from Eq. (
80
).

As a result,
s_{0}
has the following upper bound:

\displaystyle s_{0}\leq

\displaystyle~f_{0}\beta+1+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\beta(\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len})+1+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\beta(\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len})+2

where the first step follows from Eq. (
81
), the second step follows from
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]\subseteq[\mathsf{left},\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len}]
, the third step follows from the setting of
\varepsilon
.

Also,
s_{0}
has the following lower bound:

\displaystyle s_{0}\geq

\displaystyle~f_{0}\beta-1-O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\beta\mathsf{left}-1-O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\beta\mathsf{left}-2

where the first step follows from Eq. (
81
), the second step follows from
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left},\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len}]
, the third step follows from the setting of
\varepsilon
.

Combining the lower and upper bounds of
s_{0}
together, and by the definition of the set
\Theta
, we know that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}\Big(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0}\Big)\in\Theta.

Then, we show that

\displaystyle\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}\Big(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0}\Big)<\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}.

Eq. (
80
) also implies that

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0})-f_{0}\Big|\leq

\displaystyle~O(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\beta}).

(82)

Then, we have the following upper bound:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0})\leq

\displaystyle~f_{0}+O(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\beta})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+q\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}+O(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\beta})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+q\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}+O(\frac{2\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{c}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},

where the first step follows from Eq. (
82
), the second step follows from
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num},\mathsf{left}+q\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}]
, the third step follows from
\beta\in[c\frac{\mathsf{num}}{2\mathsf{len}},c\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}}]
, the forth step follows from
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/c)\leq 1
.

We also have the following lower bound:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0})\geq

\displaystyle~f_{0}-O(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\beta})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}-O(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\beta})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}-O(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{c}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}})

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},

where the first step follows from Eq. (
82
), the second step follows from
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num},\mathsf{left}+q\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}]
, the third step follows from
\beta\in[c\frac{\mathsf{num}}{2\mathsf{len}},c\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}}]
, the forth step follows from
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}/c)<1
.

Moreover, since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\geq\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},

we have that there is at most
1
element in the intersection

\displaystyle\left|\Theta\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big)\right|\leq 1.

The lemma then follows.
∎

The following claim shows that for those parts far away from the true part containing
f_{0}
, they will get no vote.

Claim L.6
.

For
\mathsf{len}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{num}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},q\in[1,\mathsf{num}]
,
let
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]
.
Let
\beta\sim\text{Uniform}([\frac{c}{2}\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}},c\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}}])
with constant
c\in(0,0.01)
. For any constant
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01\cdot c^{2})
,
let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle|z(\alpha+\beta)-z(\alpha)e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq\varepsilon|z(\alpha)|^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle\Theta=\Big\{\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s)~\Big|~s\in[\beta\mathsf{left}-10,\beta(\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len})+10]\cap\mathbb{Z}\Big\}.

Then, we have that for any
q^{\prime}\in[0,\mathsf{num}-1]
such that
|q-q^{\prime}|>1
, with probability at least
1-O(c)
,

\displaystyle\Theta\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]=\emptyset.

Proof.

Let
s_{0}
be defined as

\displaystyle s_{0}:=~\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}~\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})-2\pi f_{0}\beta+2\pi x.

By Claim
L.5
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0})\in\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big).

(83)

Then, we discuss two cases based on the range of
q^{\prime}
.

Case 1:
1<|q-q^{\prime}|<1/(4c)
.

For the ease of discussion, suppose
1<q^{\prime}-q<1/(4c)
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\geq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0}),

where the first step follows from
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the second step follows from Eq. (
83
).

Moreover, we also have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\leq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{left}+(q+\frac{1}{4c}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0})+(\frac{1}{4c}+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0})+\frac{1}{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi(s_{0}+1)),

where the second step follows from Eq. (
83
), the third step follows from
(\frac{1}{4c}+1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}<1/\beta
.

Hence, we get that
\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]
is contained in the following interval:

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0}),\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi(s_{0}+1))\Big).

Since
s_{0}
and
s_{0}+1
are two consecutive integers, by the definition of
\Theta
, there is no element of
\Theta
in this open interval. Hence, we know that in this case,

\displaystyle\Theta\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]=\emptyset.

Case 2:
|q-q^{\prime}|\geq 1/(4c)
.

For the ease of discussion, suppose that
q^{\prime}-q\geq 1/(4c)
. We have that,

\displaystyle c(q^{\prime}-q)\geq\frac{1}{4}.

(84)

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})-2\pi\beta\left(\mathsf{left}+(q-\frac{1}{2})\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\right){\pmod{2\pi}}\in\Big[-\frac{3\pi\beta\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\frac{3\pi\beta\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big],

(85)

which follows from Eq. (
83
).

We also have that,

\displaystyle\beta\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\leq c.

(86)

Then, we have that,

\displaystyle~\mathsf{Pr}\Big[2\pi\beta(\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2})\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}})-\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)}){\pmod{2\pi}}\in[-\frac{\pi\beta\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\frac{\pi\beta\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{Pr}\Big[2\pi\beta(q^{\prime}-q)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}{\pmod{2\pi}}\in[-\frac{4\pi\beta\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\frac{4\pi\beta\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\mathsf{Pr}\Big[2\pi\beta(q^{\prime}-q)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}{\pmod{2\pi}}\in[-4\pi c,+4\pi c]\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~4c+\frac{16}{q^{\prime}-q}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~100c

(87)

where the first step follows from Eq. (
85
), the second step follows from Eq. (
86
), the third step follows from Lemma
D.6
with the following parameters:

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}=

\displaystyle~{2\pi},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}=

\displaystyle~2\pi\beta(q^{\prime}-q)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle~4\pi c,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\delta}=

\displaystyle~0,

\displaystyle A=

\displaystyle~\pi c(q^{\prime}-q),

the forth step follows from Eq. (
84
).

By Eq. (
87
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Pr}\Big[\exists s_{0}\in\mathbb{Z},\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha+\beta)}{z(\alpha)})+2\pi s_{0})\in\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big]\leq 100c

As a result, we know that in this case, with probability at least
1-O(c)
,

\displaystyle\Theta\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]=\emptyset.

∎

Then, we consider
R
independent voters, i.e.,
R
significant samples
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{R}
. The following claim shows that the true part and its left and right neighbors will get at least
R
votes. Meanwhile, those parts far away from the true part will get at most
R/2
votes with high probability.

Claim L.7
.

For For
\mathsf{len}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{num}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},q\in[1,\mathsf{num}]
,
let
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]
.
Let
\beta\sim\text{Uniform}([\frac{c}{2}\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}},c\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}}])
with constant
c\in(0,0.01)
. For any constant
\varepsilon\in(0,0.01\cdot c^{2})
,
Let
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{R}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for any
i\in[R]
,

\displaystyle|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)-z(\alpha_{i})e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq\varepsilon|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}.

For any
i\in[R]
, let

\displaystyle\Theta_{i}=\Big\{\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)}{z(\alpha_{i})})+2\pi s)~\Big|~s\in[\beta\mathsf{left}-10,\beta(\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len})+10]\cap\mathbb{Z}\Big\}.

Then, it holds that:

1.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq R.

2.

For any
|q^{\prime}-q|\geq 3
, with probability at least
1-O(c)^{R/6}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\leq\frac{R}{2}.

Proof.

Part 1.

By applying Claim
L.5
, we have that,

\displaystyle\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq 1,

which implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq R.

Part 2.

By applying Claim
L.6
, we have that, for any
|q-q^{\prime}|>1
, with probability at most
O(c)
,

\displaystyle\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq 1.

By the setting of our parameter
\frac{1}{\beta}\geq\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}
, thus

\displaystyle\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|=1.

Then, for any
|q-q^{\prime}|\geq 3
, by a union bound over
q^{\prime}-1
,
q^{\prime}
, and
q^{\prime}+1
, with probability at most
O(c)
,

\displaystyle 3\geq\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq 1.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle~\Pr\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq\frac{R}{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\binom{R}{R/6}O(c)^{R/6}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\frac{eR}{R/6})^{R/6}O(c)^{R/6}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(c)^{R/6}

where the first step follows from there should be at least
0.5R/3=R/6
different
i\in[R]
satisfying
|\Theta_{i}\cap[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}]|\geq 1
,the second step follows from
\binom{n}{k}\leq(\frac{en}{k})^{k}
, the third step is straight forward.

The lemma is then proved.
∎

Finally, we consider probabilistic voters, that is, for each sample
\alpha_{i}
, with probability
1-\rho
, it is significant. The following claim shows the votes distribution in this case.

Claim L.8
.

For
\mathsf{len}\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathsf{num}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},q\in[1,\mathsf{num}]
,
let
f_{0}\in[\mathsf{left}+(q-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]
.
Let
\beta\sim\text{Uniform}([\frac{c}{2}\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}},c\cdot\frac{\mathsf{num}}{\mathsf{len}}])
with
c=\Theta(1)\in(0,0.01)
,
\varepsilon=\Theta(1)\in(0,0.01\cdot c^{2})
,
let
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{R}\in\mathbb{R}
such that for any
i\in[R]
with probability at least
1-\rho
,

\displaystyle|z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)-z(\alpha_{i})e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{0}\beta}|^{2}\leq\varepsilon|z(\alpha_{i})|^{2}.

For any
i\in[R]
, let

\displaystyle\Theta_{i}=\Big\{\frac{1}{2\pi\beta}(\arg(\frac{z(\alpha_{i}+\beta)}{z(\alpha_{i})})+2\pi s)~\Big|~s\in[\beta\mathsf{left}-10,\beta(\mathsf{left}+\mathsf{len})+10]\cap\mathbb{Z}\Big\}.

Then, it holds that

1.

With probability at least
1-O(\rho)^{R/3}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq\frac{2R}{3}.

2.

For any
|q^{\prime}-q|\geq 3
, with probability at least
1-O(c+\rho)^{R/6}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\leq\frac{R}{2}.

Proof.

Part 1.

By applying Claim
L.5
, we have that with probability at most
\rho
,

\displaystyle\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|=0,

then we have that,

\displaystyle~\Pr\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}]\Big|\leq\frac{R}{3}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\binom{R}{R/3}O(\rho)^{R/3}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\frac{eR}{R/3})^{R/3}O(\rho)^{R/3}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\rho)^{R/3}

where the first step follows from
|\Theta_{i}\cap[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}]|=0
or
1
by our parameter setting
1/\beta>3\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}

and there should be at least
R/3
different
i\in[R]
satisfying
|\Theta_{i}\cap[\mathsf{left}+(q-2)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num},\mathsf{left}+(q+1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}]|=0
,
the second step follows from
\binom{n}{k}\leq(\frac{en}{k})^{k}
, the third step is straight forward.

Part 2.

By applying Claim
L.6
, we have that, for any
|q-q^{\prime}|>1
, with probability at most
O(c)+\rho
,

\displaystyle\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+q^{\prime}\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|=1,

where the probability follows from a union bound over the success of Claim
L.6
and
\alpha_{i}
being significant.

Thus, for any
|q-q^{\prime}|\geq 3
, by a union bound, with probability at most
3((1-\rho)O(c)+\rho)=O(c+\rho)
,

\displaystyle 3\geq\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap\Big[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\frac{\mathsf{len}}{\mathsf{num}}\Big]\Big|\geq 1.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle~\Pr\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{R}\Big|\Theta_{i}\cap[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}]\Big|\geq\frac{R}{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\binom{R}{R/6}O(c+\rho)^{R/6}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~(\frac{eR}{R/6})^{R/6}O(c+\rho)^{R/6}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(c+\rho)^{R/6}

where the first step follows from there should be at least
0.5R/3=R/6
different
i\in[R]
satisfying
|\Theta_{i}\cap[\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}-2)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num},\mathsf{left}+(q^{\prime}+1)\mathsf{len}/\mathsf{num}]|\geq 1
, the second step follows from
\binom{n}{k}\leq(\frac{en}{k})^{k}
, the third step is straight forward.

∎

Appendix M
Signal Reconstruction

In this section, we wrap up all technical tools developed in previous sections and present our main result: a Fourier interpolation algorithm with improved time complexity, sample complexity, and output sparsity.

This section consists of two parts. The first part is devoted to the signal estimation. We first provide some tools that are useful for signal estimation (see Section
M.1
). Then, we formally define the heavy clusters and show their approximation property (see Section
M.2
). Next, we give a Fourier set query algorithm, which is a component in signal estimation (see Section
M.3
). We further show that it suffices to only reconstruct the signals in the bins satisfying the high SNR band condition (see Section
M.4
).

The second part focuses on the Fourier interpolation algorithm. Combining the frequency estimation algorithm in Section
L
with the signal estimation method we just developed, we obtain a Fourier interpolation algorithm with a constant success probability (see Section
M.5
). Then, we introduce the min-of-median signal estimator used to boost the success probability (see Section
M.6
). Finally, we prove our main theorem that gives a Fourier interpolation algorithm with high success probability (see Section
M.7
).

M.1
Preliminary

We provide some technical tools in this section.

The following two lemma shows that Fourier-polynomial mixed signals and Fourier-sparse signals can approximate each other.

Lemma M.1

(
[
5
]
)
.

For any
\Delta>0
,
\delta>0
, for any
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with
\sum_{j\in[k]}n_{j}=k
, let

\displaystyle x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j\in[k]}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{j}}v_{j,i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f^{\prime}_{j,i}t},

where
|f^{\prime}_{j,i}|\leq\Delta
for each
j\in[k],i\in[n_{j}]
. There exist
k
polynomials
P_{j}(t)
for
j\in[k]
of degree at most

\displaystyle d=O(T\Delta+k^{3}\log k+k\log(1/\delta))

such that

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{j\in[k]}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}P_{j}(t)-x^{*}(t)\Big\|^{2}_{T}\leq\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|^{2}_{T}.

Lemma M.2

(
[
5
, Lemma 8.8]
)
.

For any degree-
d
polynomial
Q(t)=\overset{d}{\underset{j=0}{\sum}}c_{j}t^{j}
, any
T>0
and any
\varepsilon>0
, there always exist
\gamma>0
and

\displaystyle x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{d+1}\alpha_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(\gamma j)t}

such that

|x^{*}(t)-Q(t)|\leq\varepsilon~~~\forall t\in[0,T].

Algorithm 6

Multipoint evaluation of a polynomial

1:

procedure

PolynomialEvaluation
(
P,t
)
\triangleright
Fact
M.3

2:

return

(P(t_{1}),P(t_{2}),\cdots,P(t_{d}))

\triangleright

t\in\mathbb{C}^{d}

3:

end

procedure

4:

procedure

MixedPolynomialEvaluation
(
\sum_{j=1}^{k}P_{j}(t)\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t),t
)

5:

for

j\in[k]

do

6:

v_{j}\leftarrow(P_{j}(t_{1}),P_{j}(t_{2}),\cdots,P_{j}(t_{d}))

\triangleright

t\in\mathbb{C}^{d}

7:

end

for

8:

return

(\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j,1}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t_{1}),\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j,2}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t_{2}),\cdots,\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j,3}\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t_{3}))

9:

end

procedure

The following fact shows an efficient method multi-point evaluation of a polynomial.

Fact M.3

(
[
31
, Chapter 10]
)
.

Given a degree-
d
polynomial
P(t)
, and a set of
d
locations
\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{d}\}
. There exists an algorithm that takes
O(d\log^{2}d\log\log d)
time to output the evaluations
\{P(t_{1}),P(t_{2}),\cdots,P(t_{d})\}
.

The following lemma shows the time complexity of evaluating a mixed polynomial.

Lemma M.4

(Time complexity of Algorithm
6
)
.

Procedure
MixedPolynomialEvaluation
in Algorithm
6
runs

\displaystyle O\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\max}\{d,\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\}\log^{3}({\max}\{d,\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\})\Big)

time.

Proof.

Procedure
MixedPolynomialEvaluation
in Algorithm
6
consists of the following steps:

•

In line
5
, the for loop repeats
k
times.

•

In line
6
, multipoint evaluation of a polynomial takes
d_{j}\log^{c}(d_{j})
times by Fact
M.3
, where
d_{j}={\max}\{d,\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\}
.

Hence, the total time complexity is

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}O(d_{j}\log^{3}(d_{j}))=O\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\max}\{d,\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\}\log^{3}({\max}\{d,\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\})\Big).

∎

M.2
Heavy cluster

In this section, we formally define the heavy clusters and show that using “heavy frequencies” only yields a good approximation of the ground-truth signal.

Definition M.5

(Heavy cluster)
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and
\mathcal{N}>0
. Let the filter
H
be defined as in Lemma
E.9
.
Let
\Delta_{h}=|\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{H})|
. We say a frequency
f^{*}
belongs to an
{\cal N}
-
heavy cluster
if and only if

\displaystyle\int_{f^{*}-\Delta_{h}}^{f^{*}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\cdot\mathcal{N}^{2}/k.

Claim M.6
.

Given
x^{*}(t)=\overset{k}{\underset{j=1}{\sum}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
and any
\mathcal{N}>0
. For the set of heavy frequencies:

{S^{*}}=\left\{j\in[k]\bigg|\int_{f_{j}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{j}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\cdot\mathcal{N}^{2}/k\right\},

and the signal
x_{S^{*}}(t)=\underset{j\in{S^{*}}}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\|x_{S^{*}}-x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{N}^{2}.

Proof.

Let
x_{\overline{S^{*}}}(t)=\underset{j\in[k]\backslash S^{*}}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
. Then
\|x^{*}-x_{S^{*}}\|_{T}^{2}=\|x_{\overline{S^{*}}}\|^{2}_{T}
.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle T\|x_{\overline{S^{*}}}(t)\|_{T}^{2}=

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|x_{\overline{S^{*}}}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|x_{\overline{S^{*}}}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x_{\overline{S^{*}}}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{x}_{\overline{S^{*}}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[k]\backslash S^{*}}\int_{f_{j}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{j}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{x}_{\overline{S^{*}}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[k]\backslash S^{*}}T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~T\mathcal{N}^{2},

where the first step follows from the definition of the norm, the second step follows from Lemma
E.9
Property V, the third step is straight forward, the forth step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the fifth step follows from the definition of
x_{\overline{S^{*}}}(t)
, the sixth step follows from the definition of heavy frequency, the seventh step is straightforward.

∎

M.3
Fourier set query

In this section, we present a Fourier set query algorithm such that for a Fourier-polynomial mixed signal, given all of its frequencies, the algorithm can reconstruct the signal very efficiently.

Lemma M.7
.

For
j\in[k]
, given a
d_{j}
-degree polynomial
P_{j}(t)
and a frequency
f_{j}
. Let
x_{S}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}P_{j}(t)\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t})
. Given observations of the form
x(t):=x_{S}(t)+g(t)
for arbitrary noise
g(t)
in time duration
t\in[0,T]
. Let
D:=\sum_{j=1}^{k}d_{j}
.

Then, there is an algorithm (Procedure
SignalEstimation
in Algorithm
7
) such that

•

takes
O(D\log(D))
samples from
x(t)
,

•

runs
O(D^{\omega}\log(D))
time,

•

outputs
y(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}P^{\prime}_{j}(t)\exp({2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t})
with
d
-degree polynomial
P^{\prime}_{j}(t)
, such that with probability at least
0.99
, we have

\displaystyle\|y-x_{S}\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g\|_{T}^{2}.

Proof.

By Lemma
M.2
, we have that, for all
t\in[0,T]
, there exist
D
-Fourier-sparse signals
y_{1}(t)
and
x_{S,1}(t)

\displaystyle|y(t)-y_{1}(t)|\leq\varepsilon_{1},

(88)

and

\displaystyle|x_{S}(t)-x_{S,1}(t)|\leq\varepsilon_{1}.

(89)

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-y_{1}(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\|x_{S}(t)-x_{S,1}(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\|y_{1}(t)-x_{S,1}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~2\varepsilon_{1}+\|y_{1}(t)-x_{S,1}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y_{1}(t)-x_{S,1}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

(90)

where the first step follows from
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, the second step follows from Eq. (
88
) and Eq. (
89
), the third step follows from
\varepsilon_{1}\lesssim\|y_{1}(t)-x_{S,1}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
.

We also have that

\displaystyle\|y_{1}(t)-x_{S,1}(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y_{1}(t)-y(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}+\|x_{S,1}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}+\|y(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~2\varepsilon_{1}+\|y(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}

(91)

where the first step follows from
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, the second step follows from Eq. (
88
) and Eq. (
89
), the third step follows from
\varepsilon_{1}\lesssim\|y(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}
.

By the definition of
y(t)
in line
17
in Procedure
SignalEstimation
of Algorithm
7
, we have that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}\leq\|x_{S}(t)-x(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}

(92)

We have that

\displaystyle\E[\|x-x_{S}\|_{S,w}^{2}]=

\displaystyle~\E\Big[\sum_{i\in[|S|]}w_{i}|x(t_{i})-x_{S}(t_{i})|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\E\Big[\sum_{i\in[|S|]}\frac{1}{2T|S|D(t)}|x(t_{i})-x_{S}(t_{i})|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{i\in[|S|]}\E_{t_{i}\sim D(t)}\Big[\frac{1}{2T|S|D(t)}|x(t_{i})-x_{S}(t_{i})|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~|S|\cdot\int_{-T}^{T}D(t)\frac{1}{2T|S|D(t)}|x(t)-x_{S}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-T}^{T}\frac{1}{2T}|x(t)-x_{S}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\|x(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

(93)

where the first step follows from the definition of the norm, the second step follows from the definition of
w_{i}
, the third step is straightforward, the forth follows from the definition of expectation, the fifth step follows from the definition of the norm.

We have that

\displaystyle\|y-x_{S}\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y_{1}-x_{S,1}\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y_{1}-x_{S,1}\|_{S,w}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y-x_{S}\|_{S,w}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|y-x\|_{S,w}^{2}+\|x-x_{S}\|_{S,w}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|x-x_{S}\|_{S,w}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|x-x_{S}\|_{T}^{2},

where the first step follows from Eq. (
90
), the second step follows from Lemma
J.3
, the third step follows from Eq. (
91
), the forth step follows from
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
, the fifth step follows from Eq. (
92
), the sixth step follows from Eq. (
93
) by Markov inequality with probability at least
0.99
.

∎

M.4
High signal-to-noise ratio band approximation

The goal of this section is to prove the following lemma, which roughly states that for the heavy frequencies, it suffices to only reconstruct those in the bins with high SNRs.

Lemma M.8
.

Let
x^{*}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
be the ground-truth signal and
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
be the noisy observation signal.
Let
H
be defined as in Definition
E.5
,
{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}
be defined as in Definition
C.2
with
(\sigma,b)
such that Large Offset event does not happen.
Let
U:=\{t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}~|~H(t)>1-\delta_{1}~\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\beta]\}
. Let

\displaystyle S:=\Big\{j\in[k]~\Big|~\int_{f_{j}-\Delta}^{f_{j}+\Delta}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k\Big\},

and
x_{S}(t)=\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
.

For
j\in[B]
, let
z_{j}^{*}(t):=(x^{*}\cdot H)*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
and
z_{j}(t)=(x\cdot H)*{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)
.
Let
g_{j}(t):=z_{j}(t)-z_{j}^{*}(t)
.
Let

\displaystyle S_{g1}:=\big\{j\in[B]~|~\|g_{j}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq c\|z^{*}_{j}(t)\|_{U}^{2}\big\},

(94)

where
c\in(0,0.001)
is a small universal constant.
Let

\displaystyle S_{g2}:=\left\{j\in[B]~\Bigg|~\exists f_{0}\in\{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\},~\text{and}~h_{\sigma,b}(f_{0})=j,~\text{and}~\int_{f_{0}-\Delta}^{f_{0}+\Delta}|\widehat{x^{*}\cdot H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\mathcal{N}^{2}/k\right\}.

Let
S_{g}=S_{g1}\cap S_{g2}
.
Let
S_{f}:=\{j\in[k]~|~h_{\sigma,b}(f_{j})\in S_{g}\}\cap S
and
x_{S_{f}}(t):=\underset{j\in S_{f}}{\sum}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}.

Then, we have

\displaystyle\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|{g}(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Proof.

By the definition of
S
and
S_{f}
, we have that

\displaystyle S_{f}\subseteq S.

Let
[L,R]:=U
. We have that for any
f\in S\backslash S_{f}
,
j=h_{\sigma,b}(f)
,

\displaystyle\|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\geq

\displaystyle~c\|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{U}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~c\frac{T-k^{2}(T+L-R)}{R-L}\|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~O(c)\cdot\|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2},

(95)

where the first step follows from Eq. (
94
), the second step follows from Lemma
J.9
, the third step follows from the Lemma
E.10
.

Let
{\cal T}=S\backslash S_{f}
. And for
j\in[B]
, let

\displaystyle{\cal T}_{j}:=\begin{cases}\left\{i\in S~|~h_{\sigma,b}(f_{i})=j\right\},&~\forall j\in[B]\backslash S_{g},\\
\emptyset,&~\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It is easy to see that

\displaystyle{\cal T}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{B}{\cal T}_{i}.

Moreover, by Lemma
C.4
Property I and III, the definition of
{\cal T}_{j}
and
\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)
, and the Large Offset event not happening, we have that for any
f\in\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{x}_{{\cal T}_{j}}*\widehat{H})
,

\displaystyle\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{k},

(96)

where
x_{{\cal T}_{j}}=\sum_{i\in{\cal T}_{j}}v_{i}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{i}t}
and
\widehat{x}_{{\cal T}_{j}}
is its Fourier transform.

Then, we have that

\displaystyle~T\|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\gtrsim

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{x}^{*}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{x}_{{\cal T}_{j}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{x}_{[k]\backslash{\cal T}_{j}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{x}_{{\cal T}_{j}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\gtrsim

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{x}_{{\cal T}_{j}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

(97)

where the first step follows from the definition of the norm, the second step follows from Lemma
G.3
, third step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the forth step follows from the Large Offset event not happening and the definition of
{\cal T}_{j}
, the fifth step is straight forward, the sixth step follows from Eq. (
96
).

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle~T\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~T\|x_{\cal T}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~T\|x_{\cal T}(t)\cdot H(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|x_{\cal T}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|x_{\cal T}(t)\cdot H(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{x}_{\cal T}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j=1}^{B}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\widehat{x}_{{\cal T}_{j}}(f)*\widehat{H}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]\backslash S_{g}}T\|(x^{*}(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]\backslash S_{g}}T\|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

(98)

where the first step follows from the definition of
{\cal T}
, the second step follows from
x_{\cal T}
is a
k
-Fourier-sparse signal and Lemma
E.9
Property V, the third step follows from the definition of the norm, the forth step is straight forward, the fifth step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the sixth step follows from the definition of
{\cal T}_{j}
and the Large Offset event not happened, the seventh step follows from Eq. (
97
), the eighth step follows from Eq. (
95
).

Eq. (
98
) can be upper bounded by the summation over all bins, which can be further upper bounded as follows:

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}T\cdot\|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}\int_{0}^{T}|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{g}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))\cdot\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{g}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))|^{2}\cdot\sum_{j\in[B]}|\widehat{G}^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|(\widehat{g}(f)*\widehat{H}(f))|^{2}\mathrm{d}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|({g}(t)\cdot{H}(t))|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|({g}(t)\cdot{H}(t))|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\int_{0}^{T}|{g}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~T\|{g}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

(99)

where the first step follows from the definition of the norm, the second step is straightforward, the third step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the forth step is straightforward, the fifth step follows from Lemma
C.6
, the sixth step follows from Parseval’s theorem, the seventh step follows from
g(t)=0,\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\backslash[0,T]
, the eighth step follows from Lemma
E.9
Property I, II, the ninth step follows from the definition of the norm.

Therefore, we get that

\displaystyle~T\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]\backslash S_{g}}T\|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in[B]}T\|(g(t)\cdot H(t))*G^{(j)}_{\sigma,b}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~T\|{g}(t)\|_{T}^{2},

where the first step follows from Eq. (
98
), the second step is straight forward, the third step follows from Eq. (
99
).

The lemma is then proved.

∎

M.5
Fourier interpolation with constant success probability

In this section, we give an algorithm for Fourier interpolation by combining our frequency estimation algorithm with a signal estimation algorithm. However, it only succeeds with a constant probability.

Theorem M.9
.

Let
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
x^{*}(t)\in{\cal F}_{k,F}
and
g(t)
is arbitrary noise. Given samples of
x
over
[0,T]
, there is an algorithm (Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
) that uses

\displaystyle O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

samples, runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{4\omega}\log^{2\omega+1}(k)\log^{2\omega}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

time, and outputs an
O(k^{4}\log^{4}(k/\delta))
-Fourier-sparse signal
y(t)
such that with probability at least
0.6
,

\displaystyle\|{y-x^{*}}\|_{T}\lesssim\|{g}\|_{T}+\delta\|{x^{*}}\|_{T}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{N}^{2}:=\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
be the noisy level of the observation signal.

Heavy-clusters approximation.

Let
S
be the set of heavy frequencies:

\displaystyle S=\Big\{j\in[k]\bigg|\int_{f_{j}-\Delta_{h}}^{f_{j}+\Delta_{h}}|\widehat{H\cdot x^{*}}(f)|^{2}\mathrm{d}f\geq T\cdot\mathcal{N}^{2}/k\Big\},

where
\Delta_{h}=|\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{H})|
, and let
x_{S}(t)=\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
.
By Claim
M.6
, we have

\displaystyle\|x_{S}-x^{*}\|_{T}\lesssim\mathcal{N},

(100)

which implies that it suffices to reconstruct
x_{S}
, instead of
x^{*}
.

Frequency estimation.

Conditioning on Large Offset event not happening, which holds with probability at least 0.6 by Lemma
D.8
, let
S_{f}\subseteq S
be defined as in Lemma
M.8
and
x_{S_{f}}(t)=\sum_{j\in S_{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}
. By Lemma
M.8
, we have

\displaystyle\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|{g}(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

(101)

Furthermore, by Theorem
L.2
, there is an algorithm that outputs a set of frequencies
L\subset\mathbb{R}
of size
B
such that with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(k)}
, for any
j\in S_{f}
, there exists an
\widetilde{f}\in L
such that,

\displaystyle|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}|\lesssim\Delta.

Fourier-polynomial mixed signal approximation.

We define a map
p:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow L
as follows:

\displaystyle p(f):=\arg\min_{\widetilde{f}\in L}~|f-\widetilde{f}|~~~\forall f\in\mathbb{R}.

Then,
x_{S_{f}}(t)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle x_{S_{f}}(t)=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in{S_{f}}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j}t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{j\in{S_{f}}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot p(f_{j})t}\cdot e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\cdot(f_{j}-p(f_{j}))t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\widetilde{f}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}t}\cdot\sum_{j\in{S_{f}}:~p(f_{j})=\widetilde{f}}v_{j}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}(f_{j}-\widetilde{f})t},

where the first step follows from the definition of
x_{S_{f}}
, the last step follows from interchanging the summations.

For each
\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L
, by Lemma
M.1
with
x^{*}=x_{S_{f}}
, there exists a degree
d=O(T\Delta+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)
polynomial
P_{i}(t)
such that,

\displaystyle\Big\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}\leq\sqrt{\delta}\|x_{S_{f}}(t)\|_{T}

(102)

Reconstructing the polynomials.

Define the following function family:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:=\mathrm{span}\Big\{e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}t}\cdot t^{j}~{|}~\widetilde{f}\in L,j\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}\Big\}.

Note that
\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\in{\cal F}
.

Let
D:=d\cdot|L|
. By Lemma
M.7
, there is an algorithm that runs in
O(\varepsilon^{-1}D^{\omega}\log^{3}(D)\log(1/\rho))
-time using
O(\varepsilon^{-1}D\log^{3}(D)\log(1/\rho))
samples, and outputs
y^{\prime}(t)\in{\cal F}
such that, with probability
1-\rho
,

\displaystyle\Big\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\Big\|x(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}

(103)

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\Big\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\|x(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\|g(t)\|_{T},

(104)

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from the definition of
g(t)
.

For the first term, we have that

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\Big\|_{T}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x_{S_{f}}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\sqrt{\delta}\|x_{S_{f}}(t)\|_{T}+\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\sqrt{\delta}(\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}+\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T})+\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}+\|x_{S}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|x_{S_{f}}(t)-x_{S}(t)\|_{T}+{\cal N}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|g(t)\|_{T}+{\cal N}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T},

(105)

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from Eq. (
102
), the third step follows from triangle inequality, the forth step follows is straightforward, the fifth step follows from triangle inequality, the sixth step follows from Eq. (
100
), and the last step follows from Eq. (
101
).

Hence, we get that

\displaystyle\Big\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}\leq

\displaystyle~\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\|g(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|g(t)\|_{T}+\mathcal{N}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

(106)

where the first step follows from Eq. (
104
), the second step follows from Eq. (
105
).

Transforming back to Fourier-sparse signal.

By Lemma
M.2
, we have that there is a
O(kd)
-Fourier-sparse signal
y(t)
, such that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-y^{\prime}(t)\|_{T}\leq\delta^{\prime}

(107)

where
\delta^{\prime}>0
is any positive real number. Thus,
y(t)
can be arbitrarily close to
y^{\prime}(t)
.
Moreover, the sparsity of
y(t)
is

\displaystyle O(kd)=O(k\cdot(T\Delta+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta))=O(k^{4}\log^{4}(k/\delta)),

which follows from Lemma
E.9
Property III:

\displaystyle\Delta=k\Delta_{h}=k|\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{H})|=O(k^{3}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})/T).

Moreover, we take

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=\sqrt{\|g\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}}\leq\|g\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}\|_{T}.

(108)

Therefore, the total approximation error can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-y^{\prime}(t)\|_{T}+\Big\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\Big\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\Big\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\Big\|\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\Big\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\Big\|y^{\prime}(t)-\sum_{\widetilde{f}_{i}\in L}e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}\widetilde{f}_{i}t}P_{i}(t)\Big\|_{T}+\mathcal{N}+\|g(t)\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\mathcal{N}+\|g(t)\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|g(t)\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}(t)\|_{T},

(109)

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from Eq. (
107
), the third step follows from Eq. (
105
), the forth step follows from Eq. (
106
), the fifth step follows from
\mathcal{N}=\sqrt{\|g\|_{T}^{2}+\delta\|x^{*}\|_{T}^{2}}\leq\|g\|_{T}+\sqrt{\delta}\|x^{*}\|_{T}
.

The correctness then follows by re-scaling
\delta
.

The running time of the algorithm follows from Lemma
M.10
, and the sample complexity follows from Lemma
M.11
.

The theorem is then proved.

∎

Lemma M.10

(Running time of Algorithm
8
)
.

Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{4\omega}\log^{2\omega+1}(k)\log^{2\omega}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

times.

Proof.

Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
consists of the following two steps:

•

Line
2
calls Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
. By Theorem
L.2
, it runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

time.

•

Line
3
calls Procedure
SignalEstimation
. By Lemma
M.7
, it runs in

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon^{-1}D^{\omega}\log(D)\log(1/\rho))

time, where
\varepsilon,\rho
are set to be universal constants and
D=B\cdot d
.

Following from the setting in the algorithm, we have that

\displaystyle B=

\displaystyle~O(k),

\displaystyle d=

\displaystyle~O(\Delta T+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta).

By Lemma
E.9
Property III, we have that

\displaystyle\Delta=k\Delta_{h}=k|\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{H}(f))|=O(k^{3}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})/T).

As a result, we have that

\displaystyle D=B\cdot d=O(k^{4}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1}))

(110)

Thus, the time complexity of Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
is

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))+O(\varepsilon^{-1}D^{\omega}\log(D)\log(1/\rho))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

\displaystyle~\quad+O(\varepsilon^{-1}(k^{4}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1}))^{\omega}\log(k^{4}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1}))\log(1/\rho))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{4\omega}\log^{2\omega+1}(k)\log^{2\omega}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

where the first step follows from Eq. (
110
), the second step follows from
\varepsilon=O(1),\rho=O(1),\rho_{1}=O(1)
.

∎

Lemma M.11

(Sample complexity of Algorithm
8
)
.

Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
takes

\displaystyle O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

samples.

Proof.

The sample complexity of each steps of Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
is as follows:

•

Line
2
calls Procedure
FrequencyEstimationX
. By Theorem
L.2
, it takes

\displaystyle O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

samples.

•

Line
3
calls Procedure
SignalEstimation
. By Lemma
M.7
, it takes in

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon^{-1}D\log(D)\log(1/\rho))

samples, where
\varepsilon,\rho
are set to be a universal constant and
D=B\cdot d
.

By Eq. (
110
), we have

\displaystyle D=O(k^{4}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})).

Thus, the sample complexity of Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
is

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))+O(\varepsilon^{-1}D\log(D)\log(1/\rho))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{2}\log(k)\log(k/\delta_{1})\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}/{\rho_{1}}))

\displaystyle~\quad+O(\varepsilon^{-1}(k^{4}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1}))\log(k^{4}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1}))\log(1/\rho))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

where the first step follows from Eq. (
110
), the second step follows from
\varepsilon=O(1),\rho=O(1),\rho_{1}=O(1)
.

∎

M.6
Min-of-medians signal estimator

In this section, we propose a “min-of-medians” estimator for signals that can exponentially boost the success probability.

Lemma M.12
.

Let
R_{p}\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
i\in[R_{p}]
, let
y_{i}(t)
be a signal independently sampled from some distribution such that with probability at least
0.9
,

\displaystyle\|y_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Let
y(t):=y_{j^{*}}(t)
where

j^{*}:=\underset{j\in[R_{p}]}{\arg\min}~\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y_{j}(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Then, with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(R_{p})}
,

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Proof.

Let
S=\{i~|~\|y_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}\}
. By the Chernoff bound, we have that

\displaystyle\Pr[|S|\geq 3/4R_{p}]\geq 1-2^{-\Omega(R_{p})}.

For the ease of discussion, we suppose
|S|\geq 3/4R_{p}
holds in the following proof.

Fix any
j\in S
. Then, for any
q\in S
, we have that

\displaystyle\|y_{j}(t)-y_{q}(t)\|_{T}\leq\|y_{j}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}+\|x^{*}(t)-y_{q}(t)\|_{T}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2},

(111)

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from the definition of
S
.

In other words, there are at least
|S|\geq(3/4)R_{p}
elements such that Eq. (
111
) holds. By the definition of median, we get that

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y_{j}(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

(112)

By definition of
y(t)
, we have that,

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y_{j}(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2},

(113)

where the first step follows from the definition of
y(t)
, the second step follows from Eq. (
112
).

By the definition of median, we know that there are
R_{p}/2
elements
r\in[R_{p}]
such that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-y_{r}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2},

(114)

where the last step follows from Eq. (
113
). Since
|S|\geq(3/4)R_{p}>(1/2)R_{p}
, there must exists an
r\in S
such that Eq. (
114
) holds.

As a result, we have that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-y_{r}(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\|y_{r}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\|y_{r}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2},

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from Eq. (
113
), the third step follows from the definition of
S
.

The lemma is then proved.
∎

One potential issue in applying the min-of-median signal estimator is that, we may not be able to compute the distances
\|y_{i}(t)-y_{j}(t)\|_{T}^{2}
exactly, but we can only estimate then with high accuracy. Therefore, we show that our estimator is robust with respect to approximated distances.

We first show a fact about the approximation of min and median.

Fact M.13
.

Let
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and
y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
for any
i\in[n]
,
y_{i}\in[\alpha\cdot x_{i},\beta\cdot x_{i}]
. Then, we have:

•

\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}~y_{i}\in\Big[\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}~x_{i},\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}~x_{i}\Big]
.

•

\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~y_{i}\in\Big[\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i},\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}\Big]
.

Proof.

Part 1:

Let
i^{*}=\underset{i\in[n]}{\arg\min}~y_{i}
.
We have that

\displaystyle y_{i^{*}}\geq\alpha\cdot x_{i^{*}}\geq\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}~x_{i}.

Let
j^{*}=\underset{j\in[n]}{\arg\min}~x_{j}
.
We have that

\displaystyle\underset{j\in[n]}{\min}~y_{j}\leq y_{j^{*}}\leq\beta\cdot x_{j^{*}}=\beta\cdot\underset{j\in[n]}{\min}~x_{j},

Hence,

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}~y_{i}\in\Big[\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}~x_{i},\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\min}~x_{i}\Big].

Part 2:

For any
x_{j}\leq\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}
, we have that

\displaystyle y_{j}\leq\beta\cdot x_{j}\leq\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}.

Thus,

\displaystyle|\{j\in[n]~|~y_{j}\leq\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}\}|\geq n/2,

which implies that

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~y_{i}\leq\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}.

For any
x_{j}\geq\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}
, we have that

\displaystyle y_{j}\geq\alpha\cdot x_{j}\geq\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}.

Thus,

\displaystyle|\{j\in[n]~|~y_{j}\geq\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}\}|\geq n/2,

which implies that

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~y_{i}\geq\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}.

As a result,

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~y_{i}\in\Big[\alpha\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i},\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathrm{median}}~x_{i}\Big].

∎

The following lemma shows that our min-and-median estimator can still exponentially boost the success probability given access to approximated distances.

Lemma M.14

(Robust min-of-median signal estimator)
.

Let
R_{p}\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
i\in[R_{p}]
, let
y_{i}(t)
be a signal independently sampled from some distribution such that with probability at least
0.9
,

\displaystyle\|y_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Given
d\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{R_{p}\times R_{p}}
such that for any
i,j\in[R_{p}]
,

\displaystyle d_{i,j}\in\Big[\alpha\cdot\|y_{i}(t)-y_{j}(t)\|_{T}^{2},\beta\cdot\|y_{i}(t)-y_{j}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\Big].

Let
y(t):=y_{j^{*}}(t)
where

j^{*}:=\underset{j\in[R_{p}]}{\arg\min}~\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~d_{j,i}.

Then, we have that, with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(R_{p})}
,

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Proof.

Let
S=\{i~|~\|y_{i}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}\}
. By the Chernoff bound, we have that

\displaystyle\Pr[|S|\geq 3/4R_{p}]\geq 1-2^{-\Omega(R_{p})}.

For the ease of discussion, we suppose
|S|\geq 3/4R_{p}
holds in the following proof.

Fix any
i^{*}\in S
. For any
q\in S
, we have that

\displaystyle\|y_{i^{*}}(t)-y_{q}(t)\|_{T}\leq\|y_{i^{*}}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}+\|x^{*}(t)-y_{q}(t)\|_{T}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2},

(115)

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from the definition of
S
.

By the definition of median, since
|S|>R_{p}/2
, we know that

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y_{i^{*}}(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

(116)

Then, we have that

\displaystyle\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~d_{j^{*},i}\leq

\displaystyle~\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~d_{i^{*},i}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\beta\cdot\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y_{i^{*}}(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\beta\cdot\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2},

(117)

where the first step follows from the definition of
j^{*}
, the second step follows from Fact
M.13
, the third step follows from Eq. (
116
).

Since
|S|>R_{p}/2
, by the definition of median, there must exists an
r\in S
such that

\displaystyle d_{j^{*},r}\leq\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~d_{j^{*},i}\lesssim\beta\cdot\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

(118)

As a result, we have that

\displaystyle\|y(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle~\|y(t)-y_{r}(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\|y_{r}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\alpha}v_{j^{*},r}+\|y_{r}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}+\|y_{r}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2},

where the first step follows from triangle inequality, the second step follows from the definition of
d
, the third step follows from Eq. (
118
), the forth step follows from Eq. (
117
), the fifth step follows from
r\in S
.

The proof of the lemma is then completed.
∎

M.7
Main algorithm for Fourier interpolation

In this section, we present our main theorem—a time and sample efficient Fourier interpolation algorithm with high success probability. The pseudocode is given in Algorithm
8
.

Algorithm 7

Signal estimation algorithm

1:

procedure

WeightedSketch
(
m,k,T
)

2:

c\leftarrow\Theta(\log(k)^{-1})

3:

D(t)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle D(t)\leftarrow\begin{cases}{c}\cdot(1-|t/T|)^{-1}T^{-1},&\text{ for }|t|\leq T(1-{1}/k),\\
c\cdot kT^{-1},&\text{ for }|t|\in[T(1-{1}/k),T].\end{cases}

4:

S_{0}\leftarrow m
i.i.d. samples from
D

5:

for

t\in S_{0}

do

6:

w_{t}\leftarrow\frac{1}{2T\cdot|S_{0}|\cdot D(t)}

7:

end

for

8:

Set a new distribution
D^{\prime}(t)\leftarrow w_{t}/\sum_{t^{\prime}\in S_{0}}w_{t^{\prime}}
for all
t\in S_{0}

9:

return

D^{\prime}

10:

end

procedure

11:

procedure

SignalEstimation
(
x,F,T,L
)

12:

\{f_{1},f_{2},\cdots,f_{B}\}\leftarrow L

\triangleright

L\in\mathbb{R}^{B}

13:

d\leftarrow O(\Delta T+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)

14:

s,\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{s}\},w\leftarrow\textsc{WeightedSketch}(O(Bd\log(Bd)),Bd,T)

\triangleright

w\in\mathbb{R}^{s}

15:

A_{i,B\cdot j_{2}+j_{1}}\leftarrow t_{i}^{j_{2}}\cdot\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j_{1}}t_{i})
,
A\in\mathbb{C}^{s\times B}

16:

b\leftarrow(x(t_{1}),x(t_{2}),\cdots,x(t_{s}))^{\top}

17:

Solving the following weighted linear regression

\displaystyle v^{\prime}\leftarrow\underset{v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{Bd}}{\arg\min}\|\sqrt{w}\circ(Av^{\prime}-b)\|_{2}.

18:

return

y(t)\leftarrow\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{B}\sum_{j_{2}=1}^{d}v_{B\cdot j_{2}+j_{1}}^{\prime}\cdot t^{j_{2}}\cdot\exp(2\pi\mathbf{i}f_{j_{1}}^{\prime}t)
.

19:

end

procedure

Algorithm 8

Fourier-sparse signal interpolation

1:

procedure

ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
(
x,H,G,T,F
)

2:

L\leftarrow\textsc{FrequencyEstimationX}(x,H,G,T,F)

\triangleright

L\in\mathbb{R}^{B}

3:

y(t)\leftarrow\textsc{SignalEstimation}(x,\varepsilon,k,F,T,L)

4:

return

y(t)

5:

end

procedure

6:

procedure

HighProbFourierInterpolation
(
x,H,G,T,F
)

7:

R_{p}\leftarrow\log(1/\rho)

8:

for

i\in[R_{p}]

do

9:

y_{i}(t)\leftarrow\textsc{ConstantProbFourierInterpolation}(x,H,G,T,F)

10:

end

for

11:

y(t)\leftarrow\textsc{MergeSignal}(y_{1}(t),y_{2}(t),\cdots,y_{R_{p}}(t))

12:

return

y(t)

13:

end

procedure

14:

procedure

MergeSignal
(
y_{1}(t),y_{2}(t),\cdots,y_{R_{p}}(t)
)

15:

d\leftarrow O(\Delta T+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta)

16:

for

i\in[R_{p}]

do

17:

for

j\in[R_{p}]

do

18:

s,\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{s}\},w\leftarrow\textsc{WeightedSketch}(O(Bd\log(Bd)\log(R_{p}^{2}/\rho)),2\cdot Bd,T)

19:

\triangleright

w\in\mathbb{R}^{s}

20:

S\leftarrow\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{s}\}

21:

Y\leftarrow\textsc{MixedPolynomialEvaluation}(y_{i}-y_{j},S)

\triangleright

Y\in\mathbb{C}^{s}

22:

\|y_{i}(t)-y_{j}(t)\|^{2}_{S,w}\leftarrow\sum_{l=1}^{s}w_{l}\cdot|Y_{l}|^{2}

23:

end

for

24:

\textsf{med}_{i}\leftarrow{\mathrm{median}}_{j\in[R_{p}]}\{\|y_{i}-y_{j}\|^{2}_{S,w}\}

25:

end

for

26:

i^{*}\leftarrow{\arg\min}_{i\in[R_{p}]}\{\textsf{med}_{i}\}

27:

return

y_{i^{*}}

28:

end

procedure

Theorem M.15

(Main Fourier interpolation algorithm)
.

Let
x(t)=x^{*}(t)+g(t)
, where
x^{*}
is
k
-Fourier-sparse signal with frequencies in
[-F,F]
.
Given samples of
x
over
[0,T]
, there is an algorithm (Procedure
HighProbFourierInterpolation
) uses

\displaystyle O(k^{4}\log^{6}(k/\delta)\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log(1/\rho))

samples, runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{4\omega}\log^{4\omega+2}(k/\delta)\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log^{5}(1/\rho))

time, and
outputs an
O(k^{4}\log^{4}(k/\delta))
-Fourier-sparse signal
y(t)
such that with probability at least
1-\rho
,

\displaystyle\|{y-x^{*}}\|_{T}\lesssim\|{g}\|_{T}+\delta\|{x^{*}}\|_{T}.

Proof.

We first prove the correctness of the algorithm.

Let

\displaystyle D(t):=\begin{cases}{c}\cdot(1-|t/T|)^{-1}T^{-1},&\text{ for }|t|\leq T(1-{1}/k),\\
c\cdot kT^{-1},&\text{ for }|t|\in[T(1-{1}/k),T].\end{cases}

Let
y_{1}(t),\cdots,y_{R_{p}}(t)
be the outputs of
R_{p}
independent runs of Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
. By Theorem
M.9
, we have that for any
j\in[R_{p}]
with probability at least
0.9
,

\displaystyle\|y_{j}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Let
S=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{s}\}
be
s=O(k\log(k)\log(R_{p}^{2}/\rho))
i.i.d. samples from
D(t)
, and let
w_{i}=1/(TsD(t_{i}))
for
i\in[s]
.
By Lemma
J.3
, for any
i,j\in[R_{p}]
, with probability at least
1-\rho/R_{p}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\|y_{i}(t)-y_{j}(t)\|_{S,w}^{2}\in[1/2,3/2]\cdot\|y_{i}(t)-y_{j}(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

Let

\displaystyle j^{*}=\underset{j\in[R_{p}]}{\arg\min}~\underset{i\in[R_{p}]}{\mathrm{median}}~\|y_{j}(t)-y_{i}(t)\|_{T}^{2},

and let
y(t):=y_{j^{*}}(t)
.

By Lemma
M.14
, we have that with probability at least
1-2^{-\Omega(R_{p})}
,

\displaystyle\|y_{j}(t)-x^{*}(t)\|_{T}^{2}\lesssim\frac{3/2}{1/2}\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}\eqsim\|g(t)\|_{T}^{2}.

By setting
R_{p}=\log(1/\rho)
, we get the desired result. The correctness is then proved.

The time complexity follows from Lemma
M.18
. And the sample complexity follows from Lemma
M.19
.

The proof of the theorem is completed.
∎

In the remaining of this section, we prove the time and sample complexities of Procedure
HighProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
.

The following two lemmas show the time complexity of Procedure
MergeSignal
in Algorithm
8
, which is used to boost the success probability of Fourier interpolation algorithm.

Lemma M.16

(Time complexity of Procedure
WeightedSketch
in Algorithm
7
)
.

Procedure
WeightedSketch
in Algorithm
7
runs in

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k)\log(1/\rho))

time.

Proof.

Procedure
WeightedSketch
contains the following steps:

•

In line
4
, sampling
S_{0}
takes
O(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k)\log(1/\rho))
times.

•

In line
5
, the for loop repeats
|S_{0}|
times, and each takes
O(1)
times.

Following from the setting in the algorithm, we have that

\displaystyle|S_{0}|=O(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k)\log(1/\rho)).

So, the time complexity of Procedure
WeightedSketch
in Algorithm
7
is

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k)\log(1/\rho))+|S_{0}|\cdot O(1)=O(\varepsilon^{-2}k\log(k)\log(1/\rho)).

∎

Lemma M.17

(Time complexity of Procedure
MergeSignal
in Algorithm
8
)
.

Procedure
MergeSignal
in Algorithm
8
runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{5}\log^{6}(k)\log^{4}(1/\delta_{1})\log^{5}(1/\rho))

time.

Proof.

In each call of the Procedure
MergeSignal
in Algorithm
8
, the for loop (Line
16
) repeats
R_{p}
times, each consisting of the following steps:

•

In Line
17
, the for loop repeats
R_{p}
times and each iteration has the following steps:

–

Line
19
calls Procedure
WeightedSketch
. By Lemma
M.16
, it runs in

O(Bd\log(Bd)\log(R_{p}^{2}/\rho))

time.

–

Line
21
calls Procedure
MixedPolynomialEvaluation
. By Lemma
M.4
, it runs in

\displaystyle O\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\max}\{d^{\prime},\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\}\log^{3}({\max}\{d^{\prime},\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\})\Big)

time, where
d^{\prime}=O(Bd\log(Bd)\log(R_{p}^{2}/\rho))
and
\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})=d
.

•

Line
26
computes the median in
R_{p}\log(R_{p})
time.

Following from the parameter setting in the algorithm, we have that

\displaystyle B=

\displaystyle~O(k),

\displaystyle d=

\displaystyle~O(\Delta T+k^{3}\log k+k\log 1/\delta).

By Lemma
E.9
Property III, we have that

\displaystyle\Delta=k\Delta_{h}=k|\mathrm{supp}(\widehat{H}(f))|/T=O(k^{3}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})/T).

As a result, we have that

\displaystyle B\cdot d=O(k^{4}\log^{2}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1}))

(119)

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle{\max}\{d^{\prime},\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\}=

\displaystyle~O(Bd\log(Bd)\log(R_{p}^{2}/\rho))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(1/\rho)\log(\log(1/\rho)))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{3}(1/\delta_{1})\log^{2}(1/\rho)).

(120)

where the first step follows from the definition of
d^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})
, the second step follows from Eq. (
119
), the third step is straight forward.

So, the time complexity of Procedure
MergeSignal
in Algorithm
8
is

\displaystyle~R_{p}^{2}\cdot\left(O(Bd\log(Bd)\log(R_{p}^{2}/\rho))+O\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\max}\{d^{\prime},\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\}\log^{3}({\max}\{d^{\prime},\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\})\Big)\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle~R_{p}\cdot O(R_{p}\log(R_{p}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O\Big(R^{2}_{p}\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\max}\{d^{\prime},\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\}\log^{3}({\max}\{d^{\prime},\mathrm{deg}(P_{j})\})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(\log^{2}(1/\rho)\cdot k\cdot(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{3}(1/\delta_{1})\log^{2}(1/\rho))\log^{3}(k\log(1/\delta_{1})\log(1/\rho)))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{5}\log^{6}(k)\log^{4}(1/\delta_{1})\log^{5}(1/\rho)),

where the first step follows from Eq. (
119
), the second step follows from Eq. (
120
), the third step follows from Eq. (
120
), the forth step is straight forward.

∎

The following two lemmas show the time complexity and sample complexity of our main algorithm.

Lemma M.18

(Time complexity of the main algorithm)
.

Procedure
HighProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{4\omega}\log^{4\omega+2}(k/\delta)\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log^{5}(1/\rho))

times.

Proof.

Procedure
HighProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
consists of the following steps:

•

In Line
8
, the for loop repeats
R_{p}
times with the following step:

–

Line
9
calls Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
. By Lemma
M.10
, it runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{4\omega}\log^{2\omega+1}(k)\log^{2\omega}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

time.

•

Line
11
calls Procedure
MergeSignal
. By Lemma
M.17
, it runs in

\displaystyle O(k^{5}\log^{6}(k)\log^{4}(1/\delta_{1})\log^{5}(1/\rho))

time.

Following from the setting in the algorithm, we have that

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\delta/\mathrm{poly}(k).

(121)

So, the time complexity of Procedure
HighProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
in Algorithm
8
is

\displaystyle~R_{p}\cdot O\left(k^{4\omega}\log^{2\omega+1}(k)\log^{2\omega}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\right)

\displaystyle~+O(k^{5}\log^{c+3}(k)\log^{4}(1/\delta_{1})\log^{5}(1/\rho))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~O(k^{4\omega}\log^{2\omega+1}(k)\log^{2\omega}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log^{5}(1/\rho))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{4\omega}\log^{4\omega+2}(k/\delta)\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log^{5}(1/\rho)),

where the first step follows from
R_{p}=\log(1/\rho)
, the second step follows from Eq. (
121
).

∎

Lemma M.19

(Sample complexity of the main algorithm)
.

Procedure
HighProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
takes

\displaystyle O(k^{4}\log^{6}(k/\delta)\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log(1/\rho))

samples.

Proof.

Procedure Procedure
HighProbFourierInterpolation
in Algorithm
8
consists of the following steps:

•

In Line
8
, the for loop repeats
R_{p}
times:

–

Line
9
calls Procedure
ConstantProbFourierInterpolation
. By Lemma
M.11
, it takes

\displaystyle O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

samples.

The remaining steps do not use any new sample.

Thus, the total sample complexity is

\displaystyle~R_{p}\cdot O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{4}\log^{3}(k)\log^{2}(1/\delta_{1})\log(\log(1/\delta_{1}))\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log(1/\rho))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~O(k^{4}\log^{6}(k/\delta)\log(FT)\log({\log(FT)})\log(1/\rho)),

where the first step follows from
R_{p}=\log(1/\rho)
, the second step follows from Eq. (

).

∎
